II. 
Property Tax Expense 
2 
Q. 
Please explain how tax rates are established. 
3 
A. 
Tax rates are set by local taxing jurisdictions (e.g., school district, city, 
4 
county, town, special district) for each tax year.  Each jurisdiction 
5 
determines its revenue from all sources other than property taxes, such as 
6 
state aid, sales tax revenue, and user fees.  Those revenues are subtracted 
7 
from the jurisdiction’s budget and the remainder becomes the property tax 
8 
levy.  In most cases, to determine the actual tax rate, the taxing jurisdiction 
9 
divides the tax levy by the total taxable assessed value of all property in 
10 
the jurisdiction. 
11 
 
12 
Q.   
How is the taxable value of the Company’s real property determined 
13 
for property tax purposes? 
14 
A.   
The Company pays property taxes on its real property, including land, 
15 
buildings, plant, and other equipment.  Real property owned by the 
16 
Company located in public rights-of-way is considered special franchise 
17 
property and subject to a special franchise assessment.  The preparation of 
18 
special franchise assessments is the responsibility of the Valuation 
19 
Services Division of the New York State Department of Taxation’s Office 
20 
of Real Property Tax Services (“ORPTS”), with the State Board of Real 
21 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 15 of 106 
 
Property Tax Services having the authority to approve assessments.  Real 
1 
property owned by the Company and located on private property is locally 
2 
assessed.  Regardless whether the property is subject to a special franchise 
3 
or local assessment, the methodology for determining the assessment or 
4 
taxable value of most utility property is the Reproduction Cost New Less 
5 
Depreciation (“RCNLD”) approach. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
What was the amount of property tax expense incurred by the 
8 
Company in the Historic Test Year? 
9 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit __ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, Page 1, the adjusted 
10 
property tax expense in the Historic Test Year (the twelve months ended 
11 
December 31, 2019) totaled $187.495 million for electric and $47.700 
12 
million for gas. 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
What is the forecast of property tax expense for the Rate Year and 
15 
Data Years? 
16 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit __ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, Page 2, the Company 
17 
forecasts property tax expense of $213.016 million, $229.502 million, and 
18 
$245.749 million for its electric operations in the Rate Year, Data Year 1 
19 
and Data Year 2, respectively.  As shown in Exhibit __ (RRP-5), Schedule 
20 
1, Page 2, the Company forecasts property tax expense of $54.796 million, 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 16 of 106 
 
$59.852 million, and $65.482 million for its gas operations in the Rate 
1 
Year, Data Year 1 and Data Year 2, respectively. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How do the Rate Year forecasts compare to the current allowance in 
4 
rates for property tax expense? 
5 
A. 
The Company’s forecast of $213.016 million for property tax expense for 
6 
its electric operations in the Rate Year is higher than the current fiscal year 
7 
(“FY”) 2021 allowance of $195.164 million for property tax expense.  The 
8 
Company’s forecast of $54.796 million for property tax expense for its gas 
9 
operations in the Rate Year is higher than the current FY 2021 allowance 
10 
of $47.730 million for property tax expense. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
How was the forecast of property tax expense calculated? 
13 
A. 
Because the rate filing was delayed by three months, the Company needed 
14 
to make two growth adjustments to bridge its historic property tax 
15 
expenses to the Rate Year.  The Company began with the property tax 
16 
expense incurred in FY 2020 and applied a growth factor of 1.29 percent, 
17 
which is one quarter of the annual growth rate of 5.18 percent, for the 
18 
three-month bridge period of April 1, 2020 through June 30, 2020 
19 
(“Three-Month Bridge Period”).  The Company then added forecast 
20 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 17 of 106 
 
property tax expense associated with incremental additions to plant in 
1 
service for the Three-Month Bridge Period.   
2 
 
3 
Next, the Company made an adjustment for a known change in the 
4 
obsolescence allowance to be applied to the tax roll.  The Company then 
5 
applied the annual growth factor of 5.18 percent for the period July 1, 
6 
2020 through June 30, 2021 (“Twelve-Month Bridge Period”) to calculate 
7 
forecast increases in property taxes.  The Company then added forecast 
8 
property tax expense associated with incremental additions to plant in 
9 
service for the Twelve-Month Bridge Period.  The forecast was split into 
10 
two pieces because applying a growth rate calculated for a twelve-month 
11 
period to a fifteen-month period fails to account for the growth that occurs 
12 
during the additional Three-Month Bridge Period.  This process was then 
13 
repeated for the Rate Year and Data Years.  See Exhibit __ (RRP-5), 
14 
Schedule 1, Page 3 and 4 for electric and Exhibit __ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, 
15 
Page 5 and 6 for gas.  The approach utilized by the Company is generally 
16 
consistent with the methodology used to calculate the rate allowance for 
17 
property taxes in the 2017 NMPC Rate Cases and the 2019 KEDNY and 
18 
KEDLI Rate Cases. 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 18 of 106 
 
Q. 
Please explain how the Company determined the proposed growth 
1 
factor for property taxes. 
2 
A. 
The growth factor of 5.18 percent is based on the two-year average 
3 
experienced growth rate from FY 2017 to FY 2020, as shown in Exhibit 
4 
__(SSP-1). 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
How did the Company calculate the average tax rate for the Historic 
7 
Test Year? 
8 
A. 
The Company calculated its average tax rate for the Historic Test Year in 
9 
three steps. 
10 
1.  The Company identified all tax parcels for which the Company 
11 
paid property taxes, including the location of the parcel, its 
12 
assessed value, the equalization rate of the municipality where 
13 
each parcel is located, and the taxes paid. 
14 
2.  The Company then divided the assessed value of each parcel by 
15 
the applicable equalization rate for the municipality, as 
16 
established by ORPTS, to calculate 100 percent of taxable 
17 
value for each property. 
18 
3.  Finally, the Company divided the total property taxes paid by 
19 
the result of step two above. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 19 of 106 
 
Q. 
How is the Company proposing to reflect changes in the tax rate for 
1 
the Rate Year and Data Years? 
2 
A. 
Changes in tax rates for the Rate Year and Data Years are implicit in the 
3 
5.18 percent annual growth factor.  For purposes of determining the 
4 
property taxes associated with the incremental plant additions, the Historic 
5 
Test Year’s average tax rate is used. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Was the Company’s forecast of property tax expense adjusted to 
8 
reflect savings associated with National Grid’s Accelerate Program? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  In connection with the Accelerate Program (which is described in 
10 
the direct testimony of the Company’s Revenue Requirements Panel), the 
11 
Company undertook a Property Tax Mitigation initiative.  The initiative 
12 
involved the engagement of an external consultant specializing in the areas 
13 
of valuation, compliance, and regulatory consulting.  The consultant 
14 
conducted a comprehensive review of potential areas for property tax 
15 
reductions across National Grid’s US operating companies. 
16 
 
17 
Specifically, the review examined commonly capitalized costs to identify 
18 
non-property taxable components.  Working with software vendor 
19 
PowerPlan, the Company developed programming to identify these non-
20 
taxable cost components and seeks to remove them from the reportable 
21 


--- Page 22 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 20 of 106 
 
asset base.  Removing these elements would enable the Company to 
1 
reduce reportable tax base, subject to ORPTS approval. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please explain the adjustment the Company made to the forecast to 
4 
reflect the anticipated savings from the Property Tax Mitigation 
5 
Accelerate Program initiative.

A.

10

13

14

15

16

17 
Workpaper 1.  The annual growth factor is applied to the $5.0 million

19

1

3

4

6 
Q. 
What contributes to the backcast variance? 
7 
A. 
The 5.18 percent growth factor used in the backcast was experienced 
8 
during a period when economic obsolescence decreased by 10 percent, 
9 
going from 21 percent on the 2016 assessment roll to 11 percent on the 
10 
2018 assessment roll; an average decrease of over three percent per 
11 
annum.  The obsolescence allowance on the 2016 assessment roll was a 
12 
decrease of 9 percent, going to 21 percent from 30 percent the prior year.  
13 
The difference between the 9 percent drop from the 2015 roll to the 2016 
14 
roll and the 3 percent average decrease embedded in the 5.18 percent 
15 
growth rate results in the variance between the backcast and actual 
16 
expense for FY 2017 and contributes to the overall backcast variance of 
17 
5.94 percent.

Q. 
Please describe the efforts undertaken by the Company to manage 
1 
property tax expense. 
2 
A. 
Annually, the Company reviews property assessments to determine if they 
3 
are reasonable using the RCNLD valuation method.  This method 
4 
develops what is considered a current value of the utility property and is 
5 
the method used by ORPTS for valuation.  If the actual assessments vary 
6 
substantially from the Company’s RCNLD calculations and the property 
7 
tax dollar amounts are significant, the Company will file complaints with 
8 
the applicable municipalities.  The Company makes every effort to attempt 
9 
to settle these complaints informally with the municipalities by meeting 
10 
with the assessors and municipal officials; however, if the Company is not 
11 
successful with informal negotiations, it can pursue litigation. 
12 
 
13 
The Property Tax Mitigation initiative (described above) is another effort 
14 
undertaken by the Company to lower its property tax liability and reduce 
15 
costs to customers.  Over the term of the Rate Year and Data Years 1 and 
16 
2, this initiative is projected to result in $16.6 million in savings for 
17 
Niagara Mohawk customers.  Finally, Niagara Mohawk petitions ORPTS 
18 
for an obsolescence allowance to be applied to the valuation of special 
19 
franchise property. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 26 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 24 of 106 
 
Q. 
What is obsolescence and how does it affect property taxes? 
1 
A. 
Obsolescence is an impairment in the value of an asset that is distinct from 
2 
physical depreciation.  The RCNLD methodology used by ORPTS to 
3 
develop assessments recognizes the existence of functional and economic 
4 
performance metrics.  If these metrics improve, the amount of economic 
5 
obsolescence allowed by ORPTS will decrease, causing a corresponding 
6 
increase in assessments and property taxes. 
7 
 
8

Has ORPTS accepted the Company’s applications for obsolescence 
9 
allowances? 
10 
A.

11 
shown in the table below.

13

14 
its property tax expense.  The Company’s 5.18 percent growth factor 
15 
incorporates capital spending that is approximately equal to the three-year 
16 
historic average.  As shown in Exhibit __ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, property 
17 
taxes on forecast capital spending in excess of the three-year historic 
18 
average have been separately calculated and added to the forecast property 
19 
tax expense.

Q. 
How are refunds handled and what did the Company forecast for 
1 
refunds during the Rate Year and Data Years? 
2 
A. 
Refunds are the result of successful litigation against municipalities and 
3 
are therefore non-recurring and unpredictable.  The Company does not 
4 
have a basis for forecasting refunds that may be received in the Rate Year 
5 
or Data Years.  If any refunds are obtained, the Company will petition the 
6 
Commission in accordance with NYCRR Title 16, Section 89.3 for 
7 
disposition of any refunds, as they have in the past. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing a reconciliation mechanism for property 
10 
tax expense? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  The Company currently defers 80 percent of the difference between 
12 
its actual property and special franchise tax expense and the amount 
13 
reflected in rates.  The Company is proposing to modify this mechanism.  
14 
Given the variability, uncertainty, and difficulties associated with 
15 
forecasting property tax expense, modifying the current reconciliation 
16 
mechanism to be a full two-way reconciliation is both reasonable and 
17 
appropriate.  The economic impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic on 
18 
municipal revenue, municipalities’ economic instability impacting 
19 
assessments and tax levies, difficulty in forecasting the impact of a new 
20 
rate plan on obsolescence, and other factors outside the Company’s 
21 


--- Page 29 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 27 of 106 
 
control all make forecasting property tax expense for Niagara Mohawk 
1 
particularly difficult.  Because the Company could experience property tax 
2 
expenses significantly above or below forecast levels, customers and the 
3 
Company would be better protected by a full reconciliation mechanism. 
4 
 
5 
III.  
Uncollectible Accounts Expense 
6 
Q. 
Please describe how Niagara Mohawk manages its collections 
7 
process to minimize its uncollectible expense. 
8 
A. 
Niagara Mohawk uses a full suite of collection activities and strategies to 
9 
manage its collection process and minimize uncollectible expense.  
10 
These activities and strategies are employed throughout the Company’s 
11 
delinquency management process, which consists of four key 
12 
components: 
Account 
Initiation, 
Account 
Management, 
Field 
13 
Collections, and Final Bill Management. 
14 
 
15 
Account Initiation is the point at which the Company establishes a 
16 
service contract with the customer applying for service.  Prior to 
17 
initiating service, the Company confirms the identification of each 
18 
applicant.  If an applicant has outstanding debt with the Company within 
19 
the last six years, they are required to pay the balance owed or establish a 
20 
payment agreement prior to obtaining service.  The past debt is then 
21 


--- Page 30 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 28 of 106 
 
transferred to the new active account under the terms of the new payment 
1 
agreement. 
2 
 
3 
Account Management is the method the Company uses to actively 
4 
monitor customer payment history.  The Company manages collection 
5 
accounts using a behavioral scoring mechanism referred to as a Portfolio 
6 
Management Package (“PMP”).  Each customer account in arrears is 
7 
evaluated and scored using PMP.  The output from the model, which is 
8 
reviewed at regular intervals, assists the Company in determining the 
9 
appropriate collections actions based on the customer’s past payment 
10 
behavior.  Customers are divided into five risk groups, and each group is 
11 
assigned the most cost-effective treatment path that is likely to be 
12 
successful.  In prioritizing the accounts in the portfolio, Niagara Mohawk 
13 
seeks to identify lower risk customers that will likely self-cure and 
14 
higher risk customers requiring more assertive collection measures.  This 
15 
process attempts to gear the response to the specific circumstances of the 
16 
individual customer and attempts to ensure that the most cost-effective 
17 
steps are taken to mitigate the Company’s uncollectible expense.   
18 
 
19 
Field Collections begin after a final notice has been issued to the 
20 
customer.  Field Collections are the actions taken by the Company to 
21 


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 29 of 106 
 
collect payments in a manner that is mindful of customer rights, 
1 
protections, and safety.  As a last resort, service may be terminated 
2 
because of non-payment.    
3 
 
4 
Final Bill Management is the process of collecting final or written-off 
5 
accounts with the assistance of outside collection agencies.  The 
6 
Company utilizes a tool that enables it to manage this collections work 
7 
stream and to track and execute strategies to maximize resource and 
8 
minimize costs.   
9 
 
10 
The Company also executes a residential account management strategy 
11 
directed at high arrears residential accounts.  This strategy focuses on 
12 
reaching out to customers by phone to find ways to reduce arrears and 
13 
decrease terminations using alternative methods when normal collection 
14 
paths have been unsuccessful. 
15 
 
16 
Furthermore, the Company conducts ongoing email campaigns for 
17 
residential customers with an arrears balance of at least $50 pending for 60 
18 
or more days.  Reminder emails are sent on a weekly basis to customers 
19 
that meet the criteria.  Niagara Mohawk started the campaign in mid-2016.  
20 


--- Page 32 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 30 of 106 
 
The program has resulted in approximately 220,000 emails sent to Upstate 
1 
customers annually.   
2 
 
3 
Although uncollectible expense and the Company’s mitigation efforts are 
4 
a significant focus, bad debt expense in a given year is also driven by a 
5 
number of factors outside the Company’s control – including weather, 
6 
commodity cost, customer protections, economic health, and the ongoing 
7 
COVID-19 pandemic. 
8 
 
9 
 Q. 
Please describe the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on Niagara 
10 
Mohawk’s collection practices and uncollectible expense.  
11 
A. 
The pandemic has created significant economic hardships for many 
12 
customers in the short term.  The Company continues to assess these 
13 
impacts on customers and to monitor the underlying economic conditions 
14 
in the Company’s service territory.  Because it is not possible to predict 
15 
the depth or duration of the economic downturn related to COVID-19, or 
16 
the near-term implications on customers’ ability to pay their electric and 
17 
gas bills, the Company believes a deferral mechanism is appropriate to 
18 
address growing arrears.  Contrary to the normal seasonal trends of 
19 
write-offs used to determine uncollectible recovery rates -- the level of 
20 
net write-off in FY 2021 is expected to continue to drop as a result of 
21 


--- Page 33 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 31 of 106 
 
recent statutory changes imposing an extended COVID-related 
1 
moratorium on terminations.1  Therefore, the Company anticipates an 
2 
artificially low level of write-offs and potential over-recovery of 
3 
uncollectibles in the Rate Year, followed by an elevated level of write-
4 
offs and under-recovery in the Data Years.  Thus, the Company believes 
5 
a fully reconcilable uncollectible expense deferral mechanism for both 
6 
commodity and delivery is necessary to protect customers and the 
7 
Company from significant swings in write-offs resulting from the 
8 
COVID-related collections suspension and the ongoing moratorium on 
9 
terminations.   
10 
 
11 
As discussed in more detail below, uncollectible expense includes both 
12 
commodity and delivery-related uncollectible costs.  The Company is 
13 
working with Staff and the other utilities in Case 20-M-0266 (the 
14 
“COVID-19 Proceeding”) to address the impacts of the pandemic, 
15 
including potential impacts on the Company’s collections activities and 
16 
uncollectible expense.  Because of the uncertainties associated with the 
17 
COVID-19 pandemic and how utilities will respond, the Company has 
18 
not made adjustments to its forecasts to reflect these impacts.  As part of 
19 
the generic COVID-19 Proceeding, the Company joined with the other 
20 
                                                 
1 See e.g. NY LEGIS 108 (2020), 2020 Sess. Law News of N.Y. Ch. 108 (S. 8113-A) (the “Parker 
Mosely Act”). 


--- Page 34 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 32 of 106 
 
major utilities in New York seeking Commission approval to establish a 
1 
deferral mechanism to address the impacts of COVID-19 on 
2 
uncollectible expense.  To the extent the COVID-19 Proceeding does not 
3 
provide utilities with authority to implement a fully reconcilable 
4 
uncollectible expense deferral mechanism, the Company believes this 
5 
proceeding would the proper place to more fully address the need for 
6 
such a mechanism. 
7 
 
8 
The Company has engaged in significant outreach with affected 
9 
customers during the terminations moratorium regarding their options to 
10 
use a deferred payment agreement (“DPA”) to pay bills and keep their 
11 
accounts in good standing.  The Company is committed to continuing to 
12 
engage customers in arrears to address past due charges while applying 
13 
increased flexibility to the terms of individual payment agreements.  The 
14 
Company’s residential collections activities will adhere to all 
15 
Commission policies that provide leniency to customers who come 
16 
forward with hardships.  As required by the Parker Mosley Act, the 
17 
Company will also maintain the 180-day moratorium on turn-offs for 
18 
residential customers defaulting on DPAs or with unpaid arrears, who 
19 
inform the Company that their financial circumstances changed due to 
20 
COVID-19. 
21 


--- Page 35 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 33 of 106 
 
A deferral mechanism would permit the Company to continue this level 
1 
of engagement and flexibility with customers while protecting the 
2 
Company and customers from large swings in uncollectible expense. 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
What is the Company’s recent experience with uncollectible expense 
5 
prior to COVID-19? 
6 
A. 
 The combined electric and gas uncollectible expense decreased from 
7 
$48.826 million in 2017 to $42.163 million in 2018, and then increased 
8 
slightly to $43.451 million during the Historic Test Year, as shown in 
9 
Exhibit __ (SSP-3), Schedule 1 for electric and Exhibit__(SSP-3), 
10 
Schedule 2 for gas. 
11 
 
 
12 
 
The Company’s enhanced collection processes had served to moderate 
13 
uncollectible expense during the pre-COVID period.  The uncollectible 
14 
rates were also impacted by reductions in the number of accounts 
15 
terminated for non-payment.  The short-term effect of reducing 
16 
terminations is to suppress uncollectible write-off.  The long-term effect 
17 
of reducing terminations, however, is to increase accounts receivable, 
18 
which with all else equal, in turn leads to higher uncollectible write-offs 
19 
when these accounts with larger receivable balances are eventually 
20 
written-off. 
21 


--- Page 36 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 34 of 106 
 
Q. 
Please discuss the Company’s efforts to assist low-income customers 
1 
during the collections process.  
2 
A. 
The Company strives to match low income customers with programs that 
3 
are available to assist them in paying their bills.  This includes providing 
4 
specially trained consumer advocates who aid Niagara Mohawk’s most 
5 
vulnerable customers throughout the collections process and advise them 
6 
about available programs.  These programs are further described in the 
7 
low-income section of the testimony below.  The Company also provides 
8 
training and tools to customer service representatives to prepare them to 
9 
respond to customer high bill inquires in a knowledgeable, empathetic, 
10 
and solution-oriented way.  These efforts demonstrate the Company’s 
11 
continuing commitment to assist low income customers in managing their 
12 
arrears, which, in turn, helps minimize uncollectible accounts expense. 
13

15 
Niagara Mohawk. 
16 
 A.

17

18

19 
and street lighting customers.  The rates are calculated by multiplying the

uncollectible percentage factor by the electric supply cost and the

4

5

6

the direct testimony of the Company’s Gas Rate Design Panel.

10

7 
Q.  
Why is Niagara Mohawk proposing an uncollectible rate based on the 
8 
three-year aggregated average? 
9 
A.  
Given the lack of predictability regarding the duration and extent of the 
10 
economic impacts of COVID-19, the Company believes using the 
11 
traditional methodology and three years’ of historical data to determine an 
12 
uncollectible rate is still the most appropriate basis upon which to forecast 
13 
future uncollectible expense.  Attempting to incorporate the impact of 
14 
COVID-19 into the forecasts would be largely conjecture given the current 
15 
uncertainty surrounding the future course of the COVID-19 pandemic and 
16 
its continuing impact on New York State’s economy.  Further, the 
17 
Company proposes to use a three-year aggregated average because a 
18 
multi-year average mitigates the impact of other external factors that can 
19 


--- Page 40 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 38 of 106 
 
occur in any given year.  This includes the impacts of weather and 
1 
economy, which can have an acute effect on consumption and supply cost.  
2 
Two successive warmer than normal winters of 2015-2016 and 2016-2017 
3 
contributed to lower net write-off in calendar years 2016 and 2017.  
4 
Trends in warm summer weather starting in 2016 have somewhat offset 
5 
the write-off favorability from the warm winters.  Collection process 
6 
improvements have continued to mitigate net write-off in 2018 and 2019, 
7 
signifying that a three-year aggregated average is representative of current 
8 
trends.  This has also been the standard for the recent Niagara Mohawk 
9 
rate cases.  
10 
 
11

14

by each business’ respective forecast retail revenues (tariff revenue and 
16 
late payment charge revenue), Niagara Mohawk’s uncollectible expense 
17 
for the Rate Year is $31.647 million for the electric business and $9.805 
18 
million for the gas business.  The uncollectible expense for electric and 
19 
gas for Data Years 1 and 2 are set forth in Exhibit __ (RRP-3), Schedule 
20 
31.

5 
Q. 
Does the Company have any other proposals relating to credit and 
6 
collections activities? 
7 
A.  
Yes.  The Company proposes an enhancement to the customer service 
8 
systems (CSS and CRIS) to support compliance with the federal 
9 
Telephone Consumer Protections Act (“TCPA”).  The TCPA prohibits 
10 
non-emergency autodialed calls and text messages to consumer mobile 
11 
phones, without the consumer’s express consent.  The Company’s 
12 
proposal adds the ability to identify accounts with an incorrect primary 
13 
telephone number (including through analysis of customer phone numbers 
14 
to determine the confidence level of the number being correct).  The 
15 
customer system enhancements will also allow the Company to record the 
16 
customer’s election to opt in or opt out of non-emergency communications 
17 
related to their utility service.   
18 


--- Page 43 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 41 of 106 
 
Q. 
Why does the Company believe the TCPA proposal is necessary? 
1 
A. 
This proposal is necessary to support compliance with the TCPA’s 
2 
requirements to track customer consent (and revocation of consent) and to 
3 
mitigate the Company’s exposure to litigation and claims of non-
4 
compliance.  The existing customer service systems were not designed to 
5 
track the information required by the TCPA, and lack clear options to 
6 
identify if a customer opts in or opts out of being contacted regarding 
7 
matters related to the utility service.   
8 
 
9 
The Company currently uses a manual process to identify accounts that 
10 
have incorrect telephone numbers or where the customer has revoked 
11 
consent for non-emergency calls and texts.  The enhancements will enable 
12 
check box options that can be used to quickly and accurately identify 
13 
whether customers opt in and opt out at account initiation, which will help 
14 
with customer communications and call campaigns and enhance our 
15 
ability to comply with the TCPA. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
How will the Company’s TCPA proposal benefit customers? 
18 
A. 
The addition of the checkboxes within the customer service systems will 
19 
assist in enhancing the customer experience.  By identifying wrong 
20 


--- Page 44 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 42 of 106 
 
numbers and customers who have opted out of being contacted, customers 
1 
are not subjected to unwanted non-emergency automated calls.  
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What are the costs to implement the TCPA proposal? 
4 
A. 
The portion of the project related to the cleansing of wrong numbers will 
5 
involve operating and maintenance (“O&M”) expense -- while the opt-in 
6 
and opt-out consent tracking capabilities involves system enhancements 
7 
that will be capitalized.  The total project cost across all National Grid 
8 
companies is estimated to be $1.354 million with the Niagara Mohawk 
9 
share estimated to be $0.537 million, of which $0.139 million is for 
10 
expense and $0.398 million is for capital.

12 
IV. 
Customer Information System 
13 
Q. 
Please explain the Company’s need for a new Customer Information 
14 
System (“CIS”). 
15 
A. 
National Grid’s U.S. operating companies currently utilize different 
16 
customer systems.  In New York, Niagara Mohawk and KEDLI use the 
17 
Customer Service System (“CSS”), while KEDNY utilizes the Customer 
18 
Relations Information System (“CRIS”).  These systems were 
19 
implemented decades ago and are quickly approaching obsolescence.  CSS 
20 
was originally implemented for Niagara Mohawk 21 years ago, while 
21 


--- Page 45 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 43 of 106 
 
CRIS was implemented for KEDNY 30 years ago.  Neither system can 
1 
support customers’ escalating expectations or increasingly sophisticated 
2 
billing needs, nor can they easily support services on web, mobile, and 
3 
other customer and third-party channels.  Adapting these systems to the 
4 
complex requirements of new customer programs, emerging digital 
5 
technologies, and progressive rate design is extremely difficult and time-
6 
consuming.  These legacy customer systems utilize technologies that are 
7 
largely unsupported and the required skillsets to maintain and adapt these 
8 
systems are very difficult to find in the current market.  For instance, there 
9 
are no cross-industry innovation teams working on the next release of 
10 
CSS.  A lack of modern business-configurable capabilities drives 
11 
significant dependence on IT resources for even simple business rule 
12 
configuration changes like a new program requirement.  As a result, 
13 
opportunities for customer enhancements are missed and manual 
14 
workarounds with the legacy systems abound.  A new CIS solution will 
15 
create business agility to bring new customer services and billing system 
16 
options to market quickly and deliver on National Grid’s goal of an 
17 
effortless customer experience with increased choice and flexibility. 
18 
19 


--- Page 46 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 44 of 106 
 
Q. 
Please summarize the Company’s CIS transformation proposal. 
1 
A. 
The goal of this program is to replace the current legacy CIS systems (i.e., 
2 
CSS and CRIS) with a modern, flexible application capable of performing 
3 
critical meter-to-cash processes with greater efficiency, scalability, and 
4 
extensibility for future products and services.  The new CIS will be a 
5 
shared investment among National Grid’s US electric and gas operating 
6 
companies.  The first CIS release (mid 2024) will focus on replacing the 
7 
CRIS system – used by KEDNY – because CRIS is older and has more 
8 
technology, resource, and customer service challenges.  KEDLI will also 
9 
be included in the first release because it is a gas-only company like 
10 
KEDNY, shares a common set of processes, and utilizes rate structures 
11 
that closely follow KEDNY.  The second release (mid 2026), which 
12 
establishes foundational electric functionality and integrations, will focus 
13 
on the New England electric companies and include the gas operations of 
14 
the Company’s Rhode Island affiliate, The Narragansett Electric 
15 
Company.  The CSS system will be retired in 2028 with the third and final 
16 
release when Niagara Mohawk, which has the most complex rate 
17 
structures, is anticipated to move to the new CIS.  More information 
18 
regarding the proposed implementation strategy can be found in the CIS 
19 
business case in Exhibit __ (SSP-4). 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 47 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 45 of 106 
 
The CIS transformation project will deliver advanced customer service 
1 
capabilities through integration with the Salesforce Customer Relationship 
2 
Management (“CRM”) platform being introduced by the Gas Business 
3 
Enablement (“GBE”) program, as further discussed in the direct testimony 
4 
of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel.  The new CIS will also 
5 
support and enable the advancement of customer-facing digital channel 
6 
capabilities being developed through the Customer Experience Products 
7 
(“CxP”) program, as further discussed in the direct testimony of the 
8 
Information Technology Panel.  The new CIS will also provide additional 
9 
functionality to more efficiently and effectively accommodate distributed 
10 
energy resources and to bill for non-traditional items. 
11 
 
12 
Q.  
Please describe the Company’s efforts to prepare for replacing CSS 
13 
and CRIS.  
14 
A. 
Beginning in early 2017, National Grid put together a small team to 
15 
investigate options for system replacement.  The team interviewed a 
16 
number of other U.S. gas and electric utilities that had recently 
17 
implemented a new CIS, or were moving toward a conversion, to learn 
18 
about their experiences.  An initial case for change was developed, and the 
19 
team investigated what solutions could best support future capability 
20 
needs.  In the Fall of 2018, National Grid issued a request for proposal 
21 


--- Page 48 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 46 of 106 
 
(“RFP”) to explore both Oracle and SAP software options, which after a 
1 
thorough evaluation resulted in National Grid identifying SAP Customer 
2 
Relationship & Billing (“CR&B”) as its CIS software of choice.  In early 
3 
2019, National Grid issued an RFP for services of a System Integrator.  
4 
National Grid conducted initial scoping exercises in late 2019. 
5 
 
6 
In mid-2019, National Grid introduced a “Transformation Office” 
7 
responsible 
for 
coordinating 
enterprise-wide 
initiatives. 
 
The 
8 
Transformation Office has completed a study validating that all major 
9 
programs (including CIS) are aligned to achieve the desired future state 
10 
customer experience.  This study informs each program of additional 
11 
capabilities, process changes, and people skills required.  This will enable 
12 
the CIS program to complete scoping, negotiate contracts, and begin 
13 
onboarding and training the program team. 
14 
 
15

Through the GBE program, National Grid is developing a new Salesforce-
17 
based CRM solution.  The CRM will be National Grid’s new “Customer 
18 
Engagement Center” delivering the benefits of a 360-degree view of each 
19 
customer and all related activity, optimized customer interactions with 
20 
personalization and proactive notifications through preferred channels, 
21 


--- Page 49 ---

clear visibility of the programs, products, and services used by and 
1 
available to customers to deliver on the goals of “Trust and Ease” for 
2 
customers.  The new CIS program will implement in-scope electric and 
3 
gas functionality in Salesforce, augmenting and enhancing what had 
4 
already been deployed by the GBE program. 
5

The Company expects the new CIS to provide significant customer 
8

• Full and consistent deployment of the new Salesforce CRM

11

13 
with personalization and proactive notifications through a 
14 
customer’s preferred method of communication.  As the hub of all

customer interactions, the CRM will provide a full view of all 
16 
customer attributes and activity with National Grid.  It will also 
17 
centralize customer program and service offerings, such as 
18 
payment plans, energy efficiency programs, and distributed energy 
19 
resource opportunities.

• More flexible integration to customer and business partner

4

5

6

V. 
Customer Service Quality 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s customer care function. 
5 
A. 
The centerpiece of Niagara Mohawk’s customer care function is the 
6 
Customer Call Center in Syracuse, New York.  The Customer Call Center 
7 
is a full-service center providing 24 hours per day, 365 days per year 
8 
emergency service.  The Customer Call Center staff includes 115 
9 
professional customer service representatives available to handle 
10 
emergency calls and other incoming customer inquiries such as those 
11 
related to general billing inquiries, meter reading issues, electric outage 
12 
reporting and status, and available payment options.  There is also a new 
13 
connections team staffed with 48 agents that field inquiries and follow up 
14 
on job progressions from contractors and customers for new service work. 
15 
The connections team also handles a percentage of general moves and 
16 
connect volume, supplementing the call center staff. The Company has 
17 
also entered an arrangement with two third party outsourced partners to 
18 
answer calls specific to starting and stopping service and collection-related 
19 
activities.  Those companies are IQOR located in Buffalo, New York and 
20 
NCI located in Vestal, New York. 
21 


--- Page 57 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 55 of 106 
 
 
Niagara Mohawk also has a group located in Syracuse dedicated to 
1 
handling escalated complaints received directly by the Company as well as 
2 
from the Commission.  This group, Escalated Complaint Management 
3 
Upstate New York, consists of seven full time employees dedicated to 
4 
resolving escalated customer issues, ensuring that the Company’s policies 
5 
are followed consistently, and managing the Commission’s Quick 
6 
Resolution System for the Company.   
7 
 
8 
  
Another important aspect of providing an exceptional customer experience 
9 
is obtaining feedback directly from customers.  The Company conducts 
10 
monthly telephone surveys of its residential and small to medium 
11 
commercial/industrial customers, receiving immediate feedback that the 
12 
Company has used to resolve customer issues. 
13

VI. 
Credit Card Fees 
1 
Q.   
Please describe the Company’s current options regarding payments 
2 
made using prepaid, credit, and debit cards (collectively “CC/DC”). 
3 
A.   
Currently, customers can pay their utility bills via CC/DC card in several 
4 
ways.  First, customers can pay using a CC/DC on National Grid’s website 
5 
or through the mobile app.  Second, customers enrolled in electronic 
6 
billing receive an email with a link that takes them to a site where they can 
7 
pay via CC/DC.  Finally, customers can pay by phone, using either the 
8 
automated telephone system (“IVR”) or with the assistance of a customer 
9 
representative.  Residential customers who use one of these options to 
10 
make a CC/DC payment incur a transaction fee of $2.25 for payment 
11 
amounts up to $600, and a fee of $4.50 for payment amounts greater than 
12 
$600 up to $1200.  National Grid does not currently accept recurring 
13 
CC/DC payments because customers must actively accept the vendor’s 
14 
transaction fee at the time of each transaction. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Does the Company propose to change how CC/DC payments are 
17 
handled? 
18 
A.  
Yes. The Company proposes to include in base rates the estimated cost to 
19 
recover the fees incurred by Niagara Mohawk when residential customers 
20 
make CC/DC payments.  This is referred to as a “no- fee model.”  The no-
21 


--- Page 62 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 60 of 106 
 
fee model would eliminate the per-transaction cost to customers paying by 
1 
CC/DC card by shifting that cost into base rates. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Why is Niagara Mohawk Grid making this proposal for a no-fee 
4 
model? 
5 
A. 
Credit and debit card usage has been on the rise in recent years among 
6 
payment methods.  According to a Federal Reserve Payments Study in 
7 
2018, CC/DC payments continued to show growth from 2016 to 2017 
8 
increasing 10.1 percent.  In response to surveys, customers have indicated 
9 
a preference for CC/DC payments with no fee, and the ability to setup 
10 
recurring CC/DC payments.  Customers have become accustomed to 
11 
paying without convenience fees when shopping online or paying for 
12 
services like their mobile phone.  Moving to a no-fee model will help meet 
13 
customer expectations for improved payment options and enhance 
14 
customer ease and overall experience.  In addition, the Company believes 
15 
transitioning to a no-fee model will benefit customers who receive public 
16 
assistance benefits via pre-paid debit cards.  Under the current model, such 
17 
customers can pay their utility bill with their pre-paid debit card but must 
18 
use a portion of the benefits to cover the vendor fee for CC/DC payments, 
19 
resulting in an added economic disadvantage.  Adopting a no-fee model 
20 


--- Page 63 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 61 of 106 
 
will eliminate the need for a portion of public assistance benefits to pay 
1 
this administrative fee.  
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Has the Commission approved utility proposals to shift to the no-fee 
4 
model? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  The Commission has approved similar models at New York State 
6 
Electric and Gas Corporation, Rochester Gas and Electric Corporation, 
7 
Central Hudson Gas and Electric Corporation, and Orange and Rockland 
8 
Utilities.  Consolidated Edison is seeking similar approval to utilize a no-
9 
fee model with customers.  The Company’s proposal is consistent with 
10 
proposals made by other utilities.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What are the Company’s estimated total annual costs resulting from a 
13 
transition to the no-fee model? 
14 
A. 
Using existing per transaction costs for CC/DC payments, the Company 
15 
estimates total annual costs for Niagara Mohawk associated with the new 
16 
program would be $6.519 million, $9.452 million, and $12.761 million for 
17 
the Rate Year, Data Year 1, and Data Year 2, respectively.  Details on the 
18 
calculation of estimated costs are provided in Exhibit __(SSP-7). 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 64 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 62 of 106 
 
Q. 
Does the Company anticipate seeing an increase in payments made via 
1 
CC/DC under a no-fee model? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  Based on analysis with our current third-party payment processor 
3 
and the Company’s current volumes of CC/DC payments, an increase in 
4 
CC/DC payments of 75 percent would be expected with the no-fee model 
5 
in the Rate Year, and incremental increases of 45, and 35 percent in Data 
6 
Years 1 and 2, respectively. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Does the Company believe a reconciliation mechanism is appropriate 
9 
for implementation of the no-fee model? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  Considering the uncertainty associated with the transaction fees and 
11 
a potential large increase in customers making CC/DC payments in 
12 
response to the no-fee model, the Company believes a two-way deferral 
13 
mechanism should be put in place for the no-fee model, similar to that 
14 
recently adopted for New York State Electric and Gas and Rochester Gas 
15 
& Electric in Cases 19-E-0378, et al.  If the costs to provide the no-fee 
16 
model exceed the rate allowance the Company would defer the shortfall 
17 
for future recovery from customers, or alternatively, if the costs are less 
18 
than the rate allowance, the Company would defer the excess for the 
19 
benefit of customers. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 65 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 63 of 106 
 
VII. 
Low-Income Programs 
1 
Q. 
Please summarize the Company’s approach regarding its low-income 
2 
programs. 
3 
A. 
Niagara Mohawk has a longstanding commitment to low-income 
4 
programs and continues to work proactively to protect its most vulnerable 
5 
customers.  The Company understands that low-income customers in its 
6 
service territory face substantial challenges in meeting their energy needs.  
7 
The diversity of demographics, energy usage profiles, weather, housing, 
8 
and varying cost of living across the Company’s service territory can all 
9 
impact energy affordability.  Energy costs place a larger burden on low 
10 
income families and represent a larger percentage of their income than 
11 
other households.  The Company is committed to finding the right balance 
12 
between providing benefits to those customers with the greatest need and 
13 
program costs.  The health and economic impacts of the COVID-19 
14 
pandemic are far-reaching and while further targeted residential rate relief 
15 
may be appropriate, such relief would increase costs for other customers, 
16 
including those who have also been adversely affected by the pandemic.  
17 
The Company is working with other utilities in New York in the 
18 
Commission’s generic COVID-19 proceeding, Case 20-M-0266, to 
19 
determine what, if any, additional targeted assistance should be provided 
20 


--- Page 66 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 64 of 106 
 
to the Company’s income eligible customers and those facing economic 
1 
hardship. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How have the Commission’s orders in Case 14-M-0565 affected the 
4 
Company’s low-income discount programs? 
5 
A. 
In accordance with the Commission’s “Order Adopting Low Income 
6 
Program Modifications and Directing Utility Filings,” issued May 20, 
7 
2016, in Case 14-M-0565 (the “Low Income Order”), subsequent “Order 
8 
Approving Implementation Plans with Modifications,” issued on February 
9 
17, 2017 (the “Implementation Order”), and “Order Granting in Part and 
10 
Denying in Part Requests for Reconsideration and Petitions for 
11 
Rehearing,” issued February 17, 2017, the former low-income program 
12 
has been replaced by the Energy Affordability Program (“EAP”). 
13 
 
14 
As part of implementing the Commission’s new EAP programs, utilities 
15 
are required to recalculate low-income discounts on an annual basis, to 
16 
reflect changes in average bill amounts and state median income.  While 
17 
performing the annual recalculation of tiered discounts for FY 2020, the 
18 
Company observed that significant reductions in average bills would result 
19 
in dramatic decreases in discount amounts.  To reduce potentially adverse 
20 
impacts on EAP participants from a steep drop in discount amounts, the 
21 


--- Page 67 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 65 of 106 
 
Company filed a petition with the Commission seeking approval to modify 
1 
the process for recalculating discounts to limit annual decreases to no 
2 
more than 20 percent per year.  In its “Order Approving Niagara 
3 
Mohawk’s May 2019 Petition for Authorization to Adjust the Annual 
4 
Calculation of Discount Amounts,” issued October 18, 2019, in Case 19-
5 
M-0350 (the “May 2019 Order”), the Commission approved Niagara 
6 
Mohawk’s proposed modification and adopted a 20 percent limit on 
7 
reductions in discount levels from one year to the next.  Niagara Mohawk 
8 
used the process approved in the May 2019 Order to moderate reductions 
9 
in discounts for FY 2021. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe the EAP and any other programs offered by the 
12 
Company to assist low-income customers. 
13 
A. 
Niagara Mohawk’s low-income programs include the tiered discount EAP, 
14 
which is discussed in more detail below.  The Company also offers a 
15 
Reconnect Fee Waiver Program, which will continue without change.  
16 
Additionally, the Company refers customers to the New York State 
17 
Energy Research and Development Authority’s (“NYSERDA”) EmPower 
18 
NY program.  Eligibility for all of these programs is based on a customer’s 
19 
receipt of a Home Energy Assistance Program (“HEAP”) benefit.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 68 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 66 of 106 
 
As discussed in the direct testimony of the Customer Energy Panel 
1 
(“CEP”), the energy efficiency budgets and targets include some required 
2 
allocations targeted to low and moderate income (“LMI”) customers as 
3 
directed in the “Summary of Order Authorizing Utility Energy Efficiency 
4 
and Building Electrification Portfolios through 2025,” issued January 16, 
5 
2020, in Case 18-M-0084.  Additionally, there is a low-income metric 
6 
proposed as part of the energy efficiency earnings adjustment mechanism 
7 
(“EAM”) that provides incentives for energy efficiency achievements in 
8 
excess of targets. 
9 
   
10 
The Company also utilizes consumer advocates to reach out and assist the 
11 
most vulnerable customers, such as those who are struggling to pay their 
12 
bills and those with special needs.  
13

Q. 
Who is eligible to participate in the Company’s EAP? 
15 
A. 
Niagara Mohawk customers who have received a HEAP grant are 
16 
automatically enrolled in the program when the grant is received by 
17 
Niagara Mohawk.  Customers remain in the program for 14 months 
18 
following receipt of the last HEAP grant.  Customers who are identified 
19 
through the Office of Temporary Disability Assistance (“OTDA”) file 
20 
matching mechanism, or who self-identify and provide documentation of a 
21 


--- Page 69 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 67 of 106 
 
HEAP payment paid to another vendor or utility, are also enrolled in the 
1 
program for 14 months.  The file matching process has been enhanced by 
2 
utilizing additional criteria to match customers from the OTDA file to our 
3 
customer service system, such as matching final to active accounts, as well 
4 
as matching first name, last name, and last four digits of Social Security 
5 
number, which is beyond the scope of the original process.  Additionally, 
6 
customers who are on Direct Voucher or Guaranteed (“DV/GU”) 
7 
payments through OTDA are automatically enrolled in Tier 4.  
8

Q. 
Please describe the Company’s outreach efforts to assist low-income 
4 
customers and encourage participation in the EAP. 
5 
A. 
Niagara Mohawk customers can currently only be enrolled in the EAP 
6 
upon receipt of HEAP or DV/GU payments through OTDA; therefore, 
7 
outreach efforts are focused on promoting HEAP enrollment to income-
8 
eligible customers.  This is primarily performed through outreach and 
9 
education and the Company’s consumer advocates who stress that 
10 
participation in HEAP affords a 14-month bill discount.  In addition to 
11 
HEAP and file matching mechanisms with OTDA, the Company utilizes 
12 
consumer advocates to identify additional EAP program participants.  The 
13 
advocates accomplish this through one-on-one education and outreach and 
14 
education.  Consumer advocates promote and remind customers and the 
15 
agency staff with whom they work that customers who heat with another 
16 
vendor or utility, or where heat is included in their rent, can provide proof 
17 
of receipt of HEAP to be placed on the program.  Niagara Mohawk 
18 
consumer advocates incorporate EAP information in their outreach and 
19 
educational materials, such as the Consumer Advocacy brochure, Billing 
20 
and Payment Options brochure, Financial Literacy Workbook, and a 
21 


--- Page 76 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 74 of 106 
 
HEAP Tip Sheet.  Consumer advocates also make personal outbound calls 
1 
to customers who are coded elderly, blind, and disabled and/or Life 
2 
Support and Medical Emergency who did not receive HEAP in the last 
3 
year as a way to promote HEAP and EAP participation.  The EAP 
4 
program is also promoted on the Company’s HEAP outbound calling 
5 
campaign, HEAP disconnect notice insert, and the National Grid website.  
6 
National Grid has enhanced the website to include a mechanism for 
7 
customers to send referrals to the consumer advocates via email. 
8 
 
9 
Consumer advocates identify these customers through customer referrals 
10 
made by the contact centers, field personnel, customer self-referral (e.g., 
11 
customer calls or emails to the advocates), queries, and agency referrals.  
12 
The consumer advocates make weekly, bi-monthly, and monthly visits to 
13 
selected agencies, such as the Department of Social Services, Community 
14 
Action Program agencies, Economic Opportunity Council offices, food 
15

16

17 
assisted 16,561 households and participated in 473 outreach and 
18

19

20 
households and participated in 283 outreach and educational events.

In light of the possibility that the number of customers who apply for and 
1 
receive HEAP is significantly less than the number of customers identified 
2 
as HEAP eligible, several regional advisory panels, comprised of 
3

4

8

Q. 
Is the Company requesting additional funding to increase efforts for 
14 
marketing to income eligible customers to increase participation in 
15 
the Company’s Energy Affordability Program? 
16 
A. 
Yes.   In light of the economic challenges facing our customers, including 
17 
the ongoing impacts of COVID-19, the Company believes enhanced EAP 
18 
outreach would benefit income eligible customers.  Based on the 
19 
demographic and geographic challenges in upstate New York, the 
20 
Company proposes a marketing program for the Rate Year and each of the 
21 


--- Page 78 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 76 of 106 
 
Data Years.  Niagara Mohawk will develop and implement a marketing 
1

2 
eligibility criteria to be enrolled in the Company’s EAP.  There are a 
3 
number of challenges facing the Company’s customers, including an aging 
4 
population, a higher percentage of customers living below the poverty 
5 
line, and limited rural broadband access.  There could be over 250,000 
6 
customers in upstate NY who may be candidates for HEAP, but who do 
7 
not currently receive HEAP.  Given HEAP is the main gateway into the 
8 
Company’s EAP program, increasing HEAP participation would increase

10

11

12

13 
program and budget would be designed to address the three distinct media 
14 
markets across upstate New York.  
15 
 
16 
Niagara Mohawk’s proposed EAP outreach would utilize an omnichannel 
17 
marketing approach, including mass media marketing to build baseline 
18 
awareness of HEAP.  Mass media such as print ads, radio, social media, 
19 
and digital would be utilized for proactive awareness building.  The 
20 
Company would also execute one-to-one communications with targeted 
21

Page 77 of 106 
 
customers through an enhanced multi-touchpoint approach, including a

Consumer Advocates.

Q.  
Would additional resources improve the Low Income Program 
1 
experience for the customer? 
2 
A.

VIII. Economic Development 
15 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s economic development activities and 
16 
programs. 
17 
A. 
The Company’s economic development activities support sustainable 
18 
economic growth by attracting new businesses, helping existing customers 
19 
expand their businesses, and otherwise supporting the economic 
20 
development and revitalization of Upstate New York.  The purpose of the 
21 


--- Page 83 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 81 of 106 
 
Company’s economic development activities is to help create and retain 
1 
jobs and to generate new capital investment in Niagara Mohawk’s service 
2 
territory.  The Company provides site location assistance, energy 
3 
infrastructure development, and other services to help businesses resolve 
4 
energy issues that are a barrier to growth and viability.  The Company 
5 
works closely with state, regional, county, and local economic 
6 
development agencies in providing these services.  Finally, the Company 
7 
administers a portfolio of incentive programs that support the economic 
8 
development objectives of the Company and New York State.  Those 
9 
programs include electric and gas delivery discount programs, such as the 
10 
Empire Zone Rider (“EZR”) program and Excelsior Jobs Program 
11 
(“EJP”), and grant programs that are designed to promote economic 
12 
development and urban revitalization in the Company’s service territory. 
13

1. Grant Programs 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s economic development grant 
17 
programs. 
18 
A. 
The Company presently administers a portfolio of 17 active electric grant 
19 
programs and three active gas grant programs that allow it to:  (i) help 
20 
customers solve energy issues and improve their productivity, efficiency, 
21 


--- Page 84 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 82 of 106 
 
and viability; (ii) promote sustainable “smart” growth by redeveloping 
1

2 
regional growth through the development and deployment of clean energy 
3 
technologies; and (iv) partner with state, regional, and local economic 
4 
development organizations to market the Upstate New York region to 
5 
growing companies in strategic industries. The Company has offered 
6 
economic development grant programs since 2003, during which time the 
7 
programs have received consistently strong support and recognition from 
8 
customers and state, regional and local economic development agencies 
9 
and organizations.

Q. 
How are these programs currently funded? 
12 
A. 
The electric and gas grant programs currently are funded through base rate 
13 
allowances of $11.000 million per year and $1.000 million per year, 
14 
respectively.    
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Please describe the Economic Development Grant Program deferral 
17 
mechanism. 
18 
A. 
Pursuant to the Joint Proposal in the Company’s 2017 NMPC Rate Case, 
19 
through the Economic Development Grant Program deferral, actual grant 
20 
expenditures are reconciled against amounts included in base rates, subject 
21 


--- Page 85 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 83 of 106 
 
to a downward-only reconciliation over the term of the rate plan.  Any 
1 
differences between the respective rate allowance and actual program 
2 
costs in a given rate year are carried forward and reconciled at the end of 
3 
the third rate year, with any under expenditures deferred for future use in 
4 
the respective Economic Development Grant Programs.   The Company 
5 
also may petition the Commission to utilize any deferral balances related 
6 
to the Economic Development Grant Program to fund emergency 
7 
economic assistance or other incremental economic development 
8 
programs.  In the event of any anticipated over-expenditures, the Company 
9 
may petition the Commission for deferral treatment without the obligation 
10 
to make any additional expenditure unless and until the Commission 
11 
authorizes the Company to defer amounts in excess of the three-year 
12 
aggregate rate allowance for future recovery. 
13 
 
14

15 
to the electric and gas grant programs? 
16 
A.

17 
no later than November 1 of each year the Company is required to file 
18 
with the Commission its program plan for the upcoming year.  The 
19 
Company also is required to meet annually with Department of Public 
20 


--- Page 86 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 84 of 106

1 
plan.

--- Page 88 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 86 of 106 
 
Q.   
Why is the Company proposing to include funding for workforce 
1 
development initiatives in its economic development grant program 
2 
portfolio?    
3 
A.   
Workforce development is a critical issue for economic development in 
4 
the Company’s service territory and across New York State.   The 
5 
availability of a skilled workforce is perhaps the most important factor in a 
6 
company’s site location decision.  A region or state that cannot 
7 
demonstrate that availability is at a severe disadvantage versus other 
8 
regions and states that may be competing for an economic development 
9 
project.  Similarly, existing commercial and industrial customers may 
10 
choose to expand elsewhere – or not expand at all – if the existing regional 
11 
workforce cannot adequately support their investment decision.  
12 
Therefore, the proposed grant program modifications would benefit the 
13 
regional economy and the Company’s customers through their positive 
14 
impacts on job creation, new capital investment, and higher household 
15 
income.     
16

18

20 
development and energy efficiency objectives, the Company proposes to

2.  Discount Programs 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s EZR discount program and associated 
12 
discounts. 
13 
A. 
The Company has participated in the New York State Empire Zones 
14 
program (formerly known as Economic Development Zones) since the late 
15 
1980s.  Under the EZR program contained in Rule 34 of the electric tariff 
16 
and Rule 23 of the gas tariff, the Company provides ten years of 
17 
discounted, marginal cost-based delivery rates to New York State certified 
18 
customers who add load by locating or expanding in a designated Empire 
19 
Zone.  The New York State Empire Zones program was discontinued in 
20 
2010, and no new businesses have been certified since June 30 of that 
21 


--- Page 95 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 93 of 106 
 
year.  The State, however, issued Empire Zone “retention certificates” to 
1 
certain qualifying businesses that were deemed eligible to continue 
2 
receiving Empire Zone tax benefits beyond the program sunset date.  As 
3 
of December 31, 2019, 66 electric customer accounts and 14 gas accounts 
4 
remained on the Company’s EZR program.  These electric and gas 
5 
accounts received a total of $2.798 million and $0.916 million in 
6 
discounts, respectively, during CY 2019. 
7 
 
8 
Q.  
What is the Company’s proposal with respect to the EZR program? 
9 
A.  
The Company proposes to continue providing EZR program discounts for 
10 
the duration of each currently-certified customer’s ten-year discount term 
11 
in accordance with its electric and gas tariffs.  The Company expects all 
12 
customers currently served under the EZR program to complete their 
13 
discount terms before the end of calendar year 2023.       
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please describe the EJP discount program. 
16 
A. 
The EJP was first introduced by New York State in 2010 as a replacement 
17 
for the discontinued Empire Zones program.  Like the predecessor Empire 
18 
Zones program, EJP offers state tax credits to certified new and expanding 
19 
businesses and also requires New York utilities to offer discounted electric 
20 
and gas rates to qualifying businesses.  EJP rates are based on the 
21 


--- Page 96 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 94 of 106 
 
Company’s marginal cost of providing service as discussed further in the 
1 
Electric and Gas Rate Design Panel’s testimony and are provided for a 
2 
period of up to ten years from the customer’s date of certification.   As of 
3 
December 31, 2019, there were only five electric customer accounts and 
4 
two gas accounts participating in the EJP, representing $0.055 million and 
5 
$0.002 million in annual discounts, respectively.  The Company, however, 
6 
expects that participation in the EJP program will increase as customers 
7 
continue to be certified by New York State and become eligible for EJP 
8 
discounts. 
9 
 
10

11 
efficiency?

A. 
No.

16

17 
applicable electric and gas tariff rules to include a requirement that newly 
18 
certified EJP customers explore energy efficiency opportunities through 
19

19 
A. 
Currently, the Company’s Fleet force is primarily fueled by gasoline and 
20 
diesel fuel.  Electrification of Fleet vehicles will reduce the Company’s 
21 


--- Page 101 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 99 of 106 
 
consumption of fossil fuels and facilitate compliance with federal 
1 
mandates.  Specifically, the United States Department of Energy (“DOE”) 
2 
has developed a program to reduce petroleum consumption through 
3 
targeted initiatives, such as the purchase of alternative fuel vehicles 
4 
(“AFVs”).  The program stems from the Energy Policy Act of 1992 
5 
(“EPAct”) and requires that fleet owners and gasoline suppliers purchase 
6 
AFVs, including electric or hybrid vehicles, to replace segments of its 
7 
light-duty fleet force.  This program will enable electric vehicle usage at 
8 
the Company’s facilities and facilitate the Company’s compliance with the 
9 
EPAct.  The operation of EVs and AFVs, when available, in the 
10 
Company’s service territory will reduce emissions, improve air quality, 
11 
and lower greenhouse gases.  Additionally, converting certain Fleet 
12 
vehicles to electric vehicles supports energy efficiency and carbon 
13 
reduction initiatives. 
14 
 
15 
However, prior to electrification of Fleet vehicles, the Company must 
16 
perform certain make-ready work, such as installation of EV charging 
17 
infrastructure, to ensure that its facilities can accommodate Fleet fuel 
18 
needs and can support a seamless transition from fossil fuels to electricity 
19 
for these vehicles to minimize the impact on customer service. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 102 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 100 of 106 
 
Q. 
What are the projected capital expenditures of this program? 
1 
A. 
The Company proposes to spend $0.35 million in FY 2022, $0.7 million in 
2 
each of FY 2023 and FY 2024, and $0.35 million in FY 2025 to install the 
3 
EV charging infrastructure, which is included in the Facilities capital 
4 
forecast as shown in Exhibit __ (EIOP-11). 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s proposed Facilities investments.  
7 
A. 
The Company is investing in facilities required to support the Company’s 
8 
capital and O&M programs, ensure the reliability and resilience of critical 
9 
facilities, and enhance the efficiency and productivity of office 
10 
workspaces.  The efficiency enhancements will be targeted at increasing 
11 
not only employee efficiency but also the energy efficiency of the 
12 
Company’s larger office locations.  Indeed, these programs are intended to 
13 
reduce energy usage and the carbon footprint of these buildings through a 
14 
series of renovations, including lighting improvements (e.g., installation of 
15 
LED lights) and HVAC upgrades.  In conjunction with this rate filing, the 
16 
Facilities group re-evaluated its capital plan to account for the COVID-19 
17 
pandemic and to minimize the financial impact of its investments on 
18 
customers.   
19 
 
20 


--- Page 103 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 101 of 106 
 
The Company’s Facilities investments are set forth in Exhibit __ (EIOP-
1 
11) and include a baseline Facilities plan of approximately $17.9 million 
2 
in FY 2021, $19.4 million in FY 2022, $17.0 million in FY 2023, $17.1 
3 
million in FY 2024, and $19.3 million in FY 2025 for general property 
4 
improvements and necessary upgrades including paving jobs, bathroom 
5 
and locker room upgrades, HVAC replacements, and roofing projects.  
6 
The Facilities capital investment plan also includes several larger projects 
7 
that are described below and in white papers included in Exhibit __ (SSP-
8 
11).  The total Facilities capital investment plan (inclusive of the baseline 
9 
spend and larger projects) is approximately $21.87 million in FY 2021, 
10 
$22.39 million in FY 2022, $19.96 million in FY 2023, $24.24 million in 
11 
FY 2024, and $26.91 million in FY 2025.  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Does the Company anticipate that incremental Facilities costs will be 
14 
incurred in connection with the COVID-19 pandemic? 
15 
A. 
As a result of the COVID-19 pandemic and ongoing risks and concerns, 
16 
the Facilities group made certain changes to its operational plans for the 
17 
health and safety of its employees and other parties that may visit the 
18 
Company’s offices and facilities.  Specifically, the Company added 
19 
additional cleaning protocols, provisioned PPE and other materials, 
20 
signage and workspaces.  These changes resulted in increased short-term 
21 


--- Page 104 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 102 of 106 
 
costs, some of which will be incorporated into the Company’s long-term 
1 
Facilities plan.  The Company continues to develop its Facilities approach 
2 
as information about the COVID-19 pandemic and its associated risks 
3 
emerge.  The Company intends to finalize its approach, and the associated 
4 
costs, within the next few months and will incorporate the change in costs 
5 
for the Rate Year and Data Years in its Corrections and Updates filing. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please briefly describe the Company’s significant Facilities projects. 
8 
A. 
In addition to the Fleet EV Charging Infrastructure project discussed 
9 
above, there are six large projects included in the Company’s Facilities 
10 
capital plan between FY 2021 and FY 2025 as follows: 
11 
• Critical Control Centers Project – Control centers provide critical 
12 
support to the Company’s gas and electric businesses, especially in 
13 
times of system emergencies and storms.  This project will update 
14 
and/or replace assets and equipment within five of the Company’s 
15 
control centers that are beyond or nearing the end of their useful 
16 
life or require additional support.  These improvements will 
17 
enhance resilience and reliability of the control centers, improve 
18 
their functionality and performance, and allow for the continued 
19 
delivery of safe and reliable service to customers.  Forecast capital 
20 
expenditures for this project are $1.30 million in FY 2021, $1.40 
21 


--- Page 105 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 103 of 106 
 
million for FY 2022, $1.0 million in FY 2023, $0.90 million in FY 
1 
2024, and $1.75 million in FY 2025. 
2 
• Dewey-Kensington Renovations Project – The Company will 
3 
modify and refresh employee spaces within five buildings at the 
4 
Company’s Dewey-Kensington complex in Western New York, 
5 
known as the Western Region Control Center (“WRCC”).  The 
6 
project will target training areas, service restoration rooms, and 
7 
other employee spaces to update existing equipment and replace 
8 
assets that are outdated, deteriorated due to wear and tear, or 
9 
insufficient to support intended activities.  The investments will 
10 
improve the functionality of these spaces, facilitate increased 
11 
employee performance, and enhance the safety and efficiency of 
12 
these spaces.  The Company forecasts $1.50 million in capital 
13 
expenditures in FY 2021. 
14 
• Facilities Energy Efficiency Improvement Measures – To reduce 
15 
the Company’s energy use, support the State’s carbon reduction 
16 
goals, and improve the working environment for its employees, 
17 
Niagara Mohawk will incorporate energy efficiency conservation 
18 
measures as part of future major facilities renovations.  As part of 
19 
this effort, the Company will pursue two primary projects.  First, 
20 
the Company will retrofit its Camillus operating center to achieve 
21 


--- Page 106 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 104 of 106 
 
“net-zero” energy use.  The project will incorporate solar panels, 
1 
geothermal/ground source heat pumps, façade improvements (e.g., 
2 
insulation, 
windows, 
doors), 
and 
lighting 
and 
control 
3 
improvements.  Second, the Company will undertake a lighting 
4 
retrofit of the Syracuse Office Complex (“SOC”).  The Company 
5 
will also incorporate energy efficiency conservation measures as 
6 
part of future major capital renovations.  Installation of these 
7 
energy conservation measures will reduce greenhouse gas 
8 
emissions and further support State and Company energy 
9 
efficiency and sustainability objectives.  Forecast capital 
10 
expenditures for this project are $0.50 million in FY 2021 and 
11 
$1.00 million in each of FYs 2022-2025. 
12 
• Employee EV Charging Infrastructure – This project will install 
13 
additional EV charging facilities for Company employee use at 
14 
eight office sites within its service territory.  Based on the success 
15 
of the Company’s existing employee EV charging stations, the 
16 
Company believes that installation of EV charging facilities at 
17 
these additional locations will keep pace with anticipated employee 
18 
EV growth and contribute to the State’s greenhouse gas reduction 
19 
goals.  The capital forecast for this project is approximately $0.26 
20 
million per year from FY 2021 to FY 2025. 
21 


--- Page 107 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 105 of 106 
 
• North Albany Office Renovations – The Company will replace 
1 
flooring, ceilings, the HVAC system, and other building 
2 
management systems in Building 2 at the Company’s North 
3 
Albany Facility to increase the efficiency and reduce energy 
4 
consumption at this building.  As part of this project, the Company 
5 
will also implement smart workspaces to increase employee 
6 
collaboration and productivity at this location.  Implementation of 
7 
smart workspaces will align with National Grid’s strategic smart 
8 
workplace plan.  This project will be phased over two years to 
9 
minimize the impact on employees working in the building as well 
10 
as the minimize the cost impact on customers.  The capital forecast 
11 
for this project is approximately $2.00 million each year in FY 
12 
2024 and FY 2025. 
13 
• Syracuse Office Complex Improvements – The Syracuse Office 
14 
Complex is the largest office building in Niagara Mohawk’s 
15 
service territory, serving as the primary workplace for more than 
16 
1,000 employees.  This project will provide a refresh and 
17 
reconfiguration of the existing 150,000 square foot complex to 
18 
improve workspace efficiency.  These improvements will enhance 
19 
employee productivity and collaboration, support National Grid’s 
20 
strategic smart workplace plan, and enhance the safety and security 
21 


--- Page 108 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
 
Page 106 of 106 
 
of Company employees.  The Company has adjusted the original 
1 
investment timeline to minimize the cost impact on customers in 
2 
light of the COVID-19 pandemic.  The forecast capital expenditure 
3 
for this project is approximately $0.50 million in FY 2021, and 
4 
$2.25 million each in FY 2024 and FY 2025. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Are additional Facilities investments planned for FY 2021 through FY 
7 
2025? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  National Grid is proposing a plan of facilities replacements as well 
9 
as general facilities improvements and upgrades of $43.9 million in FY 
10 
2021, $7.10 million in FY 2022, $27.6 million in FY 2023 and $7.10 
11 
million in FY 2024.  These facilities are National Grid Service Company 
12 
investments and the costs are set forth in the respective gas and electric 
13 
Revenue Requirements Panel’s Exhibit __ (RRP-11), Workpapers to 
14 
Exhibit RRP-3, Schedule 9, Workpapers 4, 7, and 10.
